**Title** (48 chars):
```
docs: capture amend-mode + scope design (0.37.4)
```

**Body**:
```
Doc-only follow-up to 0.37.3. Captures the "should vc-x1 push
do force-push sequences end-to-end" design discussion into
notes/todo.md so the framing survives the chat transcript.

Two flag additions sketched for future work:
- --scope=app|claude|both (default both) — single-repo push
- --amend — squash into @- and force-push, gated by same
  review + message approval gates. Retires the manual "Late
  changes after push" CLAUDE.md recipe once implemented.

Safety requirements for amend captured inline: force-with-lease
equivalent, review gate surfaces the rewrite, stage-prereq
checks on @-. Recommended ordering: state-sanity preflight →
stage-prereq verification → --scope → --amend.

- Cargo.toml / Cargo.lock: 0.37.3 → 0.37.4
- notes/todo.md: two new Todo entries (--scope and --amend)
  embedding the safety + ordering notes; Done line + ref [57]
- notes/chores-05.md: new 0.37.4 subsection documenting the
  rationale and pointing at the todo entries
```

OK to run `vc-x1 push main --yes ...`?

> DEVELOPER

I assume --amend should be able to be single or both and thus no obvious issues implementing --scope properly?

> AGENT

Correct on both reads:

**1. `--amend` should support single or both.** Three combinations with `--scope`:

- `--scope=app --amend` — squash + force-push app only
- `--scope=claude --amend` — squash + force-push .claude only
- `--scope=both --amend` — what we just did manually for 0.37.3

They're orthogonal flags: scope = "which repos", amend = "new commit vs squash". Full 2×2 matrix of behavior, no forbidden combinations.

**2. `--scope` done properly IS the groundwork for `--amend`.** Once the stage machine is scope-aware (per-repo commit/bookmark/push stages know whether to fire or skip), adding amend becomes per-stage behavioral variants:

- `commit-app` becomes "commit-or-squash-app", branching on `--amend`
- `push-app` becomes "push-or-force-push-app", branching on `--amend`
- Same for `-claude` counterparts

No new dispatch axis — just richer behavior inside each scope-aware stage.

**Ochid coherence works in all four combinations** because jj preserves changeIDs across squash. Both the synced case and either-side-only amend keep existing ochid trailers valid without recomputation.

One edge case worth noting (not a blocker, just a design decision):

**`--scope=app` semantics on `.claude` WC.** If user runs app-only while `.claude` has pending session writes, does push:
- (a) Ignore `.claude` WC entirely (most likely interpretation; the writes stay pending for the next `--scope=claude|both` push), or
- (b) Warn "you have `.claude` activity; consider `--scope=both`"?

Probably (a) silently — matches the "do what I said, not what you think I meant" principle. But worth deciding explicitly when implementing.

The todo entries I just added don't call out this edge case specifically. Should I fold that note in before we push 0.37.4, or leave it for the implementation discussion later?

> DEVELOPER

I say (a) but maybe a warning
What about mistakes like I've done being in .claude when I thought I was in code repo. I say tough, user mistake but I know it's going to happen and it'd be great is there was an "oh shit" revert!

> AGENT

Both great points. Folding in:

**1. Warning on scope mismatch** — when `--scope=app` is given and `.claude` has pending changes (or vice versa), print something like:
```
warn: --scope=app chosen but .claude has pending changes (N files) — they will stay pending
```
Low cost, catches the "I meant --scope=both" case before the user commits.

**2. "Oh shit" revert** — genuinely valuable, and more involved than I first thought:

The current push already does `jj op restore` on mid-flight **failure** (saves op_ids in state, restores them if anything blows up before push-app crosses the remote boundary). What you're asking is the **post-success** version: "the push worked, but I realize I did it wrong — revert."

Mechanically possible — push persists the pre-push op_ids for the failure-path; we could keep them for a bounded window after success (say, an hour) as an "undo anchor". Then `vc-x1 push --undo` (or a dedicated `vc-x1 undo-push` subcommand) would:

1. Restore both repos to the pre-push op via `jj op restore`
2. Force-push both remotes back to the pre-push ref (needs the same `--force-with-lease` safety as `--amend`)
3. Require explicit confirmation — it's destructive

Caveats to capture:
- Time-limit: undo stops working after N minutes, or after the next push (whichever first). Otherwise undo-from-yesterday becomes a footgun.
- Remote-safety: `--force-with-lease`-equivalent so it refuses if someone else pushed in between.
- Scope-aware: `vc-x1 push --undo --scope=app` undoes just the app side.

This is a distinct feature from `--amend`, so worth its own todo entry rather than folding it in.

Proposing to update this still-uncommitted 0.37.4 to include:
- Warning note added to the `--scope` todo entry
- New todo entry for "undo last push" with the safety notes
- Brief mention in the chores-05 0.37.4 subsection

Or leave 0.37.4 as-is (already has the amend+scope framing) and put these in a tiny 0.37.5 follow-up. Your call on scope discipline.

> DEVELOPER

What about leveraging "op|operation" history, maybe saving in its own dir .vc-x1-ops or ...

> AGENT

Much cleaner architectural direction, yes. Leveraging jj's op history turns undo from "push-specific retention hack" into a unified primitive.

**Sketch of `.vc-x1-ops/`:**

```
.vc-x1-ops/
  last.toml            # latest anchor across all commands
  last-push.toml       # latest push-specific anchor
  last-sync.toml       # latest sync-specific anchor
  ... (or keep one rolling log with typed entries)
```

Each anchor records:
- Timestamp + expiry
- Command name + args
- Per-repo: pre-op-id (jj op log reference)
- Per-repo: pre-push remote ref state (needed for reverting remotes that already received a push; jj op log is local-only)

**Why this is better than my earlier sketch:**

1. **Generalizes beyond push.** Sync, finalize, init, fix-desc — any mutator drops an anchor. `vc-x1 undo` works across commands.
2. **Piggybacks on jj retention.** jj's op log keeps pre-op-ids alive for ~30 days by default. We're just indexing into that, not duplicating persistence.
3. **User-visible.** `ls .vc-x1-ops/` shows what's recoverable. Discoverability via filesystem.
4. **Separates concerns from push-state.toml.** That file is "resume in-flight push" state; `.vc-x1-ops/` is "post-success undo anchor" state. Different lifetimes, different shapes.

**New complexity to flag:**

- `.gitignore` gets `/.vc-x1-ops` (new pattern; init + fixture auto-write needs updating, same discipline as `/.vc-x1`).
- Remote ref snapshots (for undo-a-successful-push) need `git show-ref` or `jj bookmark list --all-remotes` at anchor time, since jj's op log only covers local.
- Expiration / GC policy — TTL-based? N-anchors-deep ring buffer? Leave to user cleanup?
- Dual-repo coordination — push drops one anchor containing both repos' op-ids, not two separate anchors.

**Question on current commit scope:**

Three flavors of how to land this:

- (a) **Fold into 0.37.4** — expand the todo + chores entry to include `.vc-x1-ops/` design. Commit grows but stays doc-only.
- (b) **0.37.4 as currently drafted (amend + scope only), 0.37.5 for `.vc-x1-ops/`** — strict scope per commit.
- (c) **0.37.4 as currently drafted PLUS the `--scope` warning + "undo last push" mention, 0.37.5 for the `.vc-x1-ops/` architectural work** — middle ground.

My lean: **(b)**. The `.vc-x1-ops/` direction is a substantial architectural thread (new dir, new gitignore coordination, new cross-cutting module) — it deserves its own 0.37.5 todo capture commit without being mixed into 0.37.4's already-broad amend+scope framing. Keeps each commit scannable.

Your call.

> DEVELOPER

A lttle late but why --amend when jj use --squash is amend somehow significantly different, in that is will be single or both but that doesn't seem to be a big enough reason to me, but want to here why you like it.

> AGENT

Honest answer: you're right. I picked `--amend` out of git-brain habit, not from principled reasoning. `--squash` is the better name here because:

1. **Matches jj's verb.** vc-x1 is jj-adjacent tooling; consistent vocabulary within the project reads better than mixing git and jj verbs.
2. **Matches the existing manual recipe.** CLAUDE.md's "Late changes after push" literally says `jj squash --ignore-immutable`. If the flag is `--squash`, user sees the same word in the recipe and the automation.
3. **Semantically accurate for jj.** jj's squash is the native operation this flag wraps. `--amend` would obscure that by translating to git-terminology for no gain.

`--amend` is slightly more familiar to git users and slightly more specific in connotation ("modify the last commit with current changes"), but neither outweighs the consistency arguments.

**Bottom line: rename `--amend` → `--squash` in the todo entry + chores subsection.** Small edit; pre-commit so no push churn.

Should I do that rename inline before we push 0.37.4, or is this idea-stage enough that we can pick the name later when we actually implement?

> DEVELOPER

Also git uses it in merge `git merge --squash <branch-name>`:

$ git merge -h | rg squash
    --[no-]squash         create a single commit instead of doing a merge
wink@3900x 26-04-23T03:33:45.384Z:~/data/prgs/rust/vc-x1 ((main))

> AGENT

Even stronger argument — `--squash` is a name git users already know from `git merge --squash`. So renaming loses zero familiarity and gains jj-consistency. No downside.

> AGENT

Honest reconsideration — you just pulled out good counter-evidence. Summarizing the weighing:

**`--amend` arguments strengthen:**
- Your own docs already use "amend" for this exact operation (CLAUDE.md L192, chores-02 L626+, chores-03 L150). Consistency with prior text.
- `git commit --amend` is the CLOSEST semantic match — same operation, rewriting-last-commit-with-WC-content.
- Zero ambiguity.

**`--squash` has a gotcha I missed:** in git, `git commit --squash=<commit>` means something DIFFERENT from `--amend`:
- `--amend` = modify last commit with WC changes
- `--squash=<commit>` = create a fixup-marker commit for autosquash during later rebase

So a git user reading `vc-x1 push --squash` might think it does the fixup thing, not the amend thing. The context (`vc-x1 push`) probably disambiguates in practice, but the term IS overloaded in git-land.

**`--squash` arguments still stand:**
- Matches jj's native verb (`jj squash`)
- Matches CLAUDE.md "Late changes after push" recipe's actual `jj squash --ignore-immutable` command
- jj doesn't have `jj amend`; squash is the jj-native way to do this

**My honest revised lean: `--amend`**

The combination of (1) your own docs already using the word for this operation, (2) semantic precision as git-equivalent, (3) git's `--squash` meaning something else — tips me back. The jj-consistency argument is real but weaker than the three above combined.

That said, perfectly defensible either way. If you want `--squash` for jj-vocabulary discipline, I'll revert the rename. If `--amend` reads better after this data, I'll revert to that. Small edit either way.

Your call — which one?

It's tough, git has a different model of "index", "branches" and "commits" but jj doesn't have branches or an index so there is no "staging" notion, I lean towards jj's nomenclature

No further file changes needed — the rename to `--squash` is already in place. Same proposed 0.37.4 commit as the last message. OK to push?

> AGENT

Real UX concern — with multi-line bodies, the final `vc-x1 push ...` invocation gets buried under the variable assignment. Two ways to fix:

**Short-term (how I can present future pushes):** split into two Bash calls. First writes title/body to temp files; second shows just the short invocation reading from them.

```bash
# Call 1 — write message to temp files (long heredocs, but no command to see)
cat > /tmp/vc-x1-push-title.txt <<'EOF'
docs: capture squash-mode + scope design (0.37.4)
EOF
cat > /tmp/vc-x1-push-body.txt <<'EOF'
<long body…>
EOF
```
```bash
# Call 2 — the actual push, short enough to read at a glance
vc-x1 push main --yes \
  --title "$(cat /tmp/vc-x1-push-title.txt)" \
  --body "$(cat /tmp/vc-x1-push-body.txt)"
```

Works today, zero code changes. The final Bash call is immediately readable.

**Medium-term (tool-level fix):** `vc-x1 push` could take `-F PATH` / `--message-file PATH` that reads a git-style message file (first line = title, blank, rest = body). Matches `git commit -F <file>` convention. Invocation becomes:

```bash
vc-x1 push main --yes -F /tmp/msg.txt
```

Even cleaner, and removes the awkward double-variable-passing. Probably worth adding to the todo list.

Want me to:
1. Adopt the two-call pattern going forward (takes effect immediately), AND
2. Add `-F` / `--message-file` to `notes/todo.md` as a future feature?

Both are low-cost; the todo capture can piggyback on the next substantive commit.

> DEVELOPER

Why are you creating /tmp/vc-x1-push is this because the push-state.toml will be deleted because the first invocation is successful?

> AGENT

No, unrelated — those are two different things.

- **`/tmp/vc-x1-push/`** = scratch location I picked for the two-call pattern's `title.txt` and `body.txt` files. Ephemeral message scratch. Nothing to do with push-state.
- **`.vc-x1/push-state.toml`** = push's own in-repo state file. Lives at `<workspace>/.vc-x1/push-state.toml`. Created during a push, cleared on successful completion. Unrelated to the tempdir choice.

The `/tmp/vc-x1-push/` path was just me picking a namespaced tmp dir. Could equally be `/tmp/title.txt` and `/tmp/body.txt` with no subdirectory. Or `$XDG_RUNTIME_DIR/...`. Whatever.

You're right that `push-state.toml` is cleared on success — that's why I put the `ls .vc-x1/` check in Call 1 (BEFORE the push), not after. After push succeeds and state is cleared, we'd see nothing and learn nothing. Before: we can see whether state exists from a prior incomplete run.

Want me to:

1. **Proceed as sketched** — `/tmp/vc-x1-push/{title,body}.txt` scratch + pre-push state-dir check, then short Call 2 push?
2. **Different scratch location** — e.g., `/tmp/{title,body}.txt` with no subdir, or inside the project?
3. **Skip the state-dir check** — just run the two-call push cleanly and inspect `.vc-x1/` yourself before/after?

Or I misread the concern entirely — tell me what you'd prefer and I'll adjust.

> DEVELOPER

I don't care about the tmp file but how actually the second invocation is going to know where to pickup after the first invocation?

> AGENT

Ah, miscommunication — "two-call" was confusing language on my part. Let me clarify:

**"Two-call" means two BASH TOOL CALLS by me (Claude), not two `vc-x1 push` invocations.**

- **Call 1** (Bash tool): pure shell — writes `title.txt` and `body.txt` to scratch. Doesn't touch `vc-x1` at all.
- **Call 2** (Bash tool): runs `vc-x1 push main --yes --title "$(cat …)" --body "$(cat …)"` ONCE, end-to-end, same as any normal push.

`vc-x1` runs exactly one time, in Call 2. It doesn't need to know anything about Call 1 — the shell substitutes `$(cat …)` into the args BEFORE `vc-x1` starts, so by the time vc-x1 sees its args, it's just `--title "..." --body "..."` with the strings inline. Identical to passing them directly.

**`push-state.toml` is entirely unrelated to the two-call pattern.** It's the mechanism that would let you resume an INTERRUPTED single `vc-x1 push` — different concept, different purpose. We're not using it here.

So:

- Call 1 sets up scratch files.
- Call 2 does the whole push.
- `push-state.toml` gets created and cleared inside Call 2's single vc-x1 invocation, just like any normal push.

The only benefit of the two-call pattern vs. one big Bash call is **visual**: Call 2's entire text is short enough (`vc-x1 push main --yes --title "$(cat /tmp/…)" --body "$(cat /tmp/…)"`) to fit with its `Bash(...)` header visible in your terminal, instead of scrolling off.

Sound right? If yes, I'll re-run Call 1 + Call 2 as sketched.

> DEVELOPER

Got it show this will just make the Bash(...) visible to me

> AGENT

Yes — purely visual. Same push behavior, just the `vc-x1 push ...` line stays short enough to remain in view with its `Bash(...)` header.

Re-running Call 1 + Call 2 now?

> DEVELOPER

y
